It reaches a length of 1.4 meters but is not vicious unless irritated. You can also find a small pit viper, about 45 centimeters long, on the plains of eastern China. It is too small to be dangerous to a man wearing shoes. 22. There are about 27 species of rattlesnakes in the United States and Mexico. They vary in color and may or may not have spots or blotches. Some are small while others, such as the diamondbacks, may grow to 2.5 meters long. 23. There are five kinds of rattlesnakes in Central and South America, but only the tropical rattlesnake is widely distributed. The rattle on the tip of the tail is sufficient identification for a rattlesnake. 24. Most will try to escape without a fight when approached, but there is always a chance one will strike at a passerby. They do not always give a warning; they may strike first and rattle afterwards or not at all. 25. The genus Trimeresurus is a subgroup of the crotalidae. These are Asian pit vipers. These pit vipers are normally tree-loving snakes with a few species living on the ground. They basically have the same characteristics of the crotalidae–slender build and very dangerous. Their bites usually are on the upper extremities–head, neck, and shoulders. Their venom is largely hemotoxic. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 471 Survival Figure E-4: Positive Identification of Pit Vipers ELAPIDAE 26. A group of highly dangerous snakes with powerful neurotoxic venom that affects the nervou system, causing respiratory paralysis. Included in this family are coral snakes, cobras, mambas, and all the Australian venomous snakes. The coral snake is small and has caused human fatalities. The Australian death adder, tiger, taipan, and king brown snakes are among the most venomous in the world, causing many human fatalities. 27. Only by examining a dead snake can you positively determine if it is a cobra or a near relative (Figure E-5). On cobras, kraits, and coral snakes, the third scale on the upper lip touches both the nostril scale and the eye. The krait also has a row of enlarged scales down its ridged back. 28. You can find the cobras of Africa and the Near East in almost any habitat. One kind may live in or near water, another in trees. Some are aggressive and savage. The distance a cobra can strike in a forward direction is equal to the distance its head is raised above the ground. Some cobras, however, can spit venom a distance of 3 to 3.5 meters. This venom is harmless unless it gets into your eyes; then it may cause blindness if not washed out immediately. Poking around in holes and rock piles is dangerous because of the chance of encountering a spitting cobra. 472 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards Figure E-5: Positive Identification of Cobras, Draits, and Coral Snakes LATICAUDINAE AND HYDROPHIDAE 29. A subfamily of elapidae, these snakes are specialized in that they found a better environment in the oceans. Why they are in the oceans is not clear to science. 30. Sea snakes differ in appearance from other snakes in that they have an oarlike tail to aid in swimming. Some species of sea snakes have venom several times more toxic than the cobra’s. Because of their marine environment, sea snakes seldom come in contact with humans. The exceptions are fisherman who capture these dangerous snakes in fish nets and scuba divers who swim in waters where sea snakes are found. 31. There are many species of sea snakes. They vary greatly in color and shape. Their scales distinguish them from eels that have no scales. 32. Sea snakes occur in salt water along the coasts throughout the Pacific. There are also sea snakes on the east coast of Africa and in the Persian Gulf. There are no sea snakes in the Atlantic Ocean. 33. There is no need to fear sea snakes. They have not been known to attack a man swimming. Fishermen occasionally get bit by a sea snake caught in a net. The bite is dangerous. COLUBRIDAE 34. The largest group of snakes worldwide. In this family there are species that are rear-fanged; however, most are completely harmless to man. They have a venomB-GL-382-006-FP-001 473 Survival producing gland and enlarged, grooved rear fangs that allow venom to flow into the wound. The inefficient venom apparatus and the specialized venom is effective on coldblooded animals (such as frogs and lizards) but not considered a threat to human life. The boomslang and the twig snake of Africa have, however, caused human deaths. Viperidae Elapidae Common adder Long-nosed adder Gaboon viper Levant viper Horned desert viper McMahon’s viper Mole viper Palestinian viper Puff adder Rhinoceros viper Russell’s viper Sand viper Saw-scaled viper Ursini’s viper Australian copperhead Crotalidae Boomslang Bush viper Bushmaster Cottonmouth Eastern diamondback rattlesnake Eyelash pit viper Fer-de-lance Green tree pit viper Habu pit viper Jumping viper Malayan pit viper Mohave Rattlesnake Pallas’ viper Tropical rattlesnake Wagler’s pit viper Common cobra Coral snake Death adder Egyptian cobra Green mamba King cobra Krait Taipan American copperhead Western diamondback rattlesnake Hydrophildae Tiger snake Banded sea snake Yellow-bellied sea snake Figure E-6: Groupings of Snakes SECTION 4 LIZARDS 35. There is little to fear from lizards as long as you follow the same precautions as for avoiding snakebite. Usually, there are only two poisonous lizards: the Gila monster and the Mexican beaded lizard. The venom of both these lizards is neurotoxic. The two lizards are in the same family, and both are slow moving with a docile nature. 36. The komodo dragon (Varanus komodoensis), although not poisonous, can be dangerous due to its large size. These lizards can reach lengths of 3 meters and weigh over 115 kilograms. Do not try to capture this lizard. 474 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards SECTION 5 POISONOUS SNAKES OF THE AMERICAS AMERICAN COPPERHEAD Agkistrodon contortrix 37. Description. Chestnut color dominates overall, with darker crossbands of rich browns that become narrower on top and widen at the bottom. The top of the head is a coppery color. 38. Characteristic. Very common over much of its range, with a natural camouflage ability to blend in the environment. Copperheads are rather quiet and inoffensive in disposition but will defend themselves vigorously. Bites occur when the snakes are stepped on or when a victim is lying next to one. A copperhead lying on a bed of dead leaves becomes invisible. Its venom is hemotoxic. 39. Habitat. Found in wooded and rocky areas and mountainous regions. 40. Length. Average 60 centimeters, maximum 120 centimeters. 41. Distribution. Eastern Gulf States, Texas, Arkansas, Maryland, North Florida, Illinois, Oklahoma, Kansas, Ohio, New York, Alabama, Tennessee, and Massachusetts. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 475 Survival 476 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards BUSHMASTER Lachesis mutus 42. Description. The body hue is rather pale brown or pinkish, with a series of large bold dark brown or black blotches extending along the body. Its scales are extremely rough. 43. Characteristics. The World's largest pit viper has a bad reputation. This huge venom– ous snake is not common anywhere in its range. It lives in remote and isolated habitats and is largely nocturnal in its feeding habits; it seldom bites anyone, so few bites are recorded. A bite from one would indeed be very serious and fatal if medical aid was not immediately available. Usually, the bites occur in remote, dense jungles, many kilometers and several hours or even days away from medical help. Bushmaster fangs are long. In large bushmasters, they can measure 3.8 centimeters. Its venom is a powerful hemotoxin. 44. Habitat. Found chiefly in tropical forests in their range. 45. Length. Average 2.1 meters, maximum 3.7 meters. 46. Distribution. Nicaragua, Costa Rica, Panama, Trinidad, and Brazil. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 477 Survival 478 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards CORAL SNAKE Micrurus fulvius 47. Description. Beautifully marked with bright blacks, reds, and yellows. To identify the species, remember that when red touches yellow it is a coral snake. 48. Characteristics. Common over range, but secretive in its habits, therefore seldom seen. It has short fanges that are fixed in an erect position. It often chews to release its venom into a wound. Its venom is very powerful. The venom is neurotoxic, causing respiratory paralysis in the victim, who succumbs to suffocation. 49. Habitat. Found in a variety of habitats including wooded areas, swamps, palmetto and scrub areas. Coral snakes often venture into residential locations. 50. Length. Average 60 centimeters, maximum 115 centimeters. 51. Distribution. Southeast North Carolina, Gulf States, west central Mississippi, Florida, Florida Keys, and west to Texas. Another genus of coral snake is found in Arizona. Coral snakes are also found throughout Central and most South America. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 479 Survival 480 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards COTTONMOUTH Agkistrodon piscivorus 52. Description. Colors are variable. Adults are uniformly olive brown or black. The young and subadults are strongly crossbanded with dark brown. 53. Characteristics. These dangerous semiaquatic snakes closely resemble harmless water snakes that have the same habitat. Therefore, it is best to leave all water snakes alone. Cottonmouths often stand their ground. An aroused cottonmouth will draw its head close to its body and open its mouth showing its white interior. Cottonmouth venom is hemotoxic and potent. Bites are prone to gangrene. 54. Habitat. Found in swamps, lakes, rivers, and ditches. 55. Length. Average 90 centimeters, maximum 1.8 meters. 56. Distribution. Southeast Virginia, west central Alabama, south Georgia, Illinois, east central Kentucky, south central Oklahoma, Texas, North and South Carolina, Florida, and the Florida Keys. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 481 Survival 482 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards EASTERN DIAMONDBACK RATTLESNAKE Crotalus adamanteus 57. Description. Diamonds are dark brown or black, outlined by a row of cream or yellowish scales. Ground color is olive to brown. 58. Characteristics. The largest venomous snake in the United States. Large individual snakes can have fangs that measure 2.5 centimeters in a straight line. This species has a sullen disposition, ready to defend itself when threatened. Its venom is potent and hemotoxic, causing great pain and damage to tissue. 59. Habitat. Found in palmettos and scrubs, swamps, pine woods, and flatwoods. It has been observed swimming many miles out in the Gulf of Mexico, reaching some of the islands off the Florida coast. 60. Length. Average 1.4 meters, maximum 2.4 meters. 61. Distribution. Coastal areas of North Carolina, South Carolina, Louisiana, Florida, and the Florida Keys. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 483 Survival 484 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards EYELASH PIT VIPER Bothrops schlegeli 62. Description. Identified by several spiny scales over each eye. Color is highly variable, from bright yellow over its entire body to reddish-yellow spots throughout the body. 63. Characteristics. Arboreal snake that seldom comes to the ground. It feels more secure in low-hanging trees where it looks for tree frogs and birds. It is a dangerous species because most of its bites occur on the upper extremities. It has an irritable disposition. It will strike with little provocation. Its venom is hemotoxic, causing severe tissue damage. Deaths have occurred from the bites of these snakes. 64. Habitat. Tree-loving species found in rain forests; common on plantations and in palm trees. 65. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 75 centimeters. 66. Distribution. Southern Mexico, throughout Central America, Columbia, Ecuador, and Venezuela. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 485 Survival 486 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards FER-DE-LANCE Bothrops atrox 67. man. There are several closely related species in this group. All are very dangerous to 68. Description. Variable coloration, from gray to olive, brown, or reddish, with dark triangles edged with light scales. Triangles are narrow at the top and wide at the bottom. 69. Characteristics. This highly dangerous snake is responsible for a high mortality rate. It has an irritable disposition, ready to strike with little provocation. The female fer-de-lance is highly prolific, producing up to 60 young born with a dangerous bite. The venom of this species is hemotoxic, painful, and hemorrhagic (causing profuse internal bleeding). The venom causes massive tissue destruction. 70. Habitat. Found on cultivated land and farms, often entering houses in search of rodents. 71. Length. Average 1.4 meters, maximum 2.4 meters. 72. Distribution. Southern Mexico, throughout Central and South America. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 487 Survival 488 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards JUMPING VIPER Bothrops nummifer 73. Description. It has a stocky body. Its ground color varies from brown to gray and it has dark brown or black dorsal blotches. It has no pattern on its head. 74. Characteristics. It is chiefly a nocturnal snake. It comes out in the early evening hours to feed on lizards, rodents, and frogs. As the name implies, this species can strike with force as it actually leaves the ground. Its venom is hemotoxic. Humans have died from the bites inflicted by large jumping vipers. They often hide under fallen logs and piles of leaves and are difficult to see. 75. Habitat. Found in rain forests, on plantations, and on wooded hillsides. 76. Length. Average 60 centimeters, maximum 120 centimeter. 77. Distribution. Southern Mexico, Honduras, Guatemala, Costa Rica, Panama, and El Salvador. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 489 Survival 490 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards MOJAVE RATTLESNAKE Crotalus scutulatus 78. Description. This snake’s entire body is a pallid or sandy odor with darker diamondshaped markings bordered by Iighter-colored scales and black bands around the tail. 79. Characteristics. Although this rattlesnake is of moderate size, its bite is very serious. Its venom has quantities of neurotoxic elements that affect the central nervous system. Deaths have resulted from this snake’s bite. 80. Habitat. Found in arid regions, deserts, and rocky hillsides from sea level to 2400-meter elevations. 81. Length. Average 75 centimeters, maximum 1.2 meters. 82. Distribution. Mojave Desert in California, Nevada, southwest Arizona, and Texas into Mexico. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 491 Survival 492 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards TROPICAL RATTLESNAKE Crotalus terrificus 83. Description. Coloration is light to dark brown with a series of darker rhombs or diamonds bordered by a buff color. 84. Characteristics. Extremely dangerous with an irritable disposition, ready to strike with little or no warning (use of its rattle). This species has a highly toxic venom containing neurotoxic and hemotoxic components that paralyze the central nervous system and cause great damage to tissue. 85. Habitat. Found in sandy places, plantations, and dry hillsides. 86. Length. Average 1.4 meters, maximum 2.1 meters. 87. Distribution. Southern Mexico, Central America, and Brazil to Argentina. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 493 Survival 494 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards WESTERN DIAMONDBACK RATTLESNAKE Crotalus atrox 88. Description. The body is a light buff color with darker brown diamond-shaped markings. The tail has heavy black and white bands. 89. Characteristics. This bold rattlesnake holds its ground. When coiled and rattling, it is ready to defend itself. It injects a large amount of venom when it bites, making it one of the most dangerous snakes. Its venom is hemotoxic, causing considerable pain and tissue damage. 90. Habitat. It is a very common snake over its range. It is found in grasslands, deserts, woodlands, and canyons. 91. Length. Average 1.5 meters, maximum 2 meters. 92. Distribution. Southeast California, Oklahoma, Texas, New Mexico, and Arizona. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 495 Survival 496 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards SECTION 6 POISONOUS SNAKES OF EUROPE COMMON ADDER Vipera berus 93. Description. Its color is variable. Some adult specimens are completely black while others have a dark zigzag pattern running along the back. 94. Characteristics. The common adder is a small true viper that has a short temper and often strikes without hesitation. Its venom is hemotoxic, destroying blood cells and causing tissue damage. Most injuries occur to campers, hikers, and field workers. 95. Habitat. Common adders are found in a variety of habitats, from grassy fields to rocky slopes, and on farms and cultivated lands. 96. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 60 centimeters. 97. Distribution. Very common throughout most of Europe. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 497 Survival 498 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards LONG-NOSED ADDER Vipera ammodytes 98. Description. Coloration is gray, brown, or reddish with a dark brown or black zigzag pattern running the length of its back. A dark stripe is usually found behind each eye. 99. Characteristics. A small snake commonly found in much of its range. The term “long-nosed” comes frorm the projection of tiny scales Iocated on the tip of its nose. This viper is responsible for many bites. Deaths have been recorded. Its venom is hemotoxic, causing severe pain and massive tissue damage. The rate of survival is good with medical aid. 100. Habitat. Open fields, cultivated lands, farms, and rocky slopes. 101. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 90 centimeters. 102. Distribution. Italy, Yugoslavia, northern Albania, and Romania. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 499 Survival 500 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards JOHN H. TASHJIAN/BERND VON SCHROEDER PALLAS' VIPER Agkistrodon halys 103. Description. Coloration is gray, tan, or yellow, with markings similar to those of the American copperhead. 104. Characteristics. This snake is timid and rarely strikes. Its venom is hemotoxic but rarely fatal. 105. Habitat. Found in open fields, hillsides, and farming regions. 106. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 90 centimeters. 107. Distribution. Throughout southeastern Europe. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 501 Survival 502 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards JOHN H. TASHJIAN/BORJE FLARDH URSINI'S VIPER Vipera ursinii 108. Description. The common adder, long-nosed adder, and Ursini’s viper basically have the same coloration and dorsal zigzag pattern. The exception among these adders is that the common adder and Ursini’s viper Iack the projection of tiny scales on the tip of the nose. 109. Characteristics. These little vipers have an irritable disposition. They will readily strike when approached. Their venom is hemotoxic. Although rare, deaths from the bites of these vipers have been recorded. 110. Habitat. Meadows, farmlands, rocky hillsides, and open, grassy fields. 111. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 90 centimeters. 112. Distribution. Most of Europe, Greece, Germany, Yugoslavia, France, Italy, Hungary, Romania, Bulgaria, and Albania. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 503 Survival 504 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards SECTION 7 POISONOUS SNAKES OF AFRICA AND ASIA JOHN H. JASHUIAN/CALIFORNIA ACADEMY OF SCIENCE BOOMSLANG Dispholidus typus 113. Description. Coloration varies but is generally green or brown, which makes it very hard to see in its habitat. 114. Characteristics. Will strike if molested. Its venom is hemotoxic; even small amounts cause severe hemorrhaging, making it dangerous to man. 115. Habitat. Found in forested areas. It will spend most of its time in trees or looking for chameleons and other prey in bushes. 116. Length. Generally less than 60 centimeters. 117. Distribution. Found throughout sub-Saharan Africa. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 505 Survival 506 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards BUSHVIPER Atheris squamiger 118. Description. Often called leaf viper, its color varies from ground colors of pale green to olive, brown, or rusty brown. It uses it prehensile tail to secure itself to branches. 119. Characteristics. An aboreal species that often comes down to the ground to feed on small rodents. It is not aggressive, but it will defend itself when molested or touched. Its venom is hemotoxic; healthy adults rarely die from its bite. 120. Habitat. Found in rain forests and woodlands bordering swamps and forests. Often found in trees, low-hanging branches, or brush. 121. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 75 centimeters. 122. Distrubition. Most of Africa, Angola, Cameroon, Uganda, Kenya, and Zaire. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 507 Survival 508 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards COMMON COBRA Naja naja 123. Description. Also known as the Asiatic cobra. Usually slate gray to brown overall. The back of the hood may or may not have a pattern. 124. Characteristics. A very common species responsible for many deaths each year. When aroused or threatened, the cobra will lift its head off the ground and spread its hood, making it more menacing. Its venom is highly neurotoxic, causing respiratory paralysis with some tissue damage. The cobra would rather retreat if possible, but if escape is shut off, it will be a dangerous creature to deal with. 125. Habitat. Found in any habitat cultivated farms, swamps, open fields, and human dwelling where it searches for rodents. 126. Length. Average 1.2 meters, maximum 2.1 meters. 127. Distribution. All of Asia. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 509 Survival 510 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards EGYPTIAN COBRA Naja haje 128. Description. Yellowish, dark brown, or black uniform top with brown crossbands. Its head is sometimes black. 129. Characteristics. It is extremely dangerous. It is responsible for many human deaths. Once aroused or threatened, it will attack and continue the attack until it feels an escape is possible. Its venom is neurotoxic and much stronger than the common cobra. Its venom causes paralysis and death due to respiratory failure. 130. Habitat. Cultivated farmlands, open fields, and arid countrysides. It is often seen around homes searching for rodents. 131. Length. Average 1.5 meters, maximum 2.5 meters. 132. Distribution. Africa, Iraq, Syria, and Saudi Arabia. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 511 Survival 512 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards GABOON VIPER Bitis gabonica 133. Description. Pink to brown with a vertebral series of elongated yellowish or light brown spots connected by hourglass-shaped markings on each side. It has a dark brown stripe behind each eye. This dangerous viper is almost invisible on the forest floor. A 1.8-meter-long Gaboon viper could weigh 16 kilograms. 134. Characteristics. The largest and heaviest of all true vipers, having a very large triangular head. It comes out in the evening to feed. Fortunately, it is not aggressive, but it will stand its ground if approached. It bites when molested or stepped on. Its fangs are enormous, often measuring 5 centimeters long. It injects a large amount of venom when it strikes. Its venom is neurotoxic and hemotoxic. 135. Habitat. Dense rain forests. Occasionally found in open country. 136. Length. Average 1.2 meters, maximum 1.8 meters. 137. Distribution. Most of Africa. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 513 Survival 514 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards GREEN MAMBA Dendraspis angusticeps 138. Description. Most mambas are uniformly bright green over their entire body. The black mamba, the largest of the species, is uniformly olive to black. 139. Characteristics. The mamba is the dreaded snake species of Africa. Treat it with great respect. It is considered one of the most dangerous snakes known. Not only is it highly venomous but it is aggressive and its victim has little chance to escape from a bite. Its venom is highly neurotoxic. 140. Habitat. Mambas are at home in brush, trees, and low-hanging branches looking for birds, a usual diet for this species. 141. Length. Average 1.8 meters, maximum 3.7 meters. 142. Distribution. Most of Africa. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 515 Survival 516 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards GREEN TREE PIT VIPER Trimeresurus gramineus 143. Description. Uniform bright or dull green with Iight yellow on the facial lips. 144. Characteristics. A small arboreal snake of some importance, though not considered a deadly species. It is a dangerous species because most of its bites occur in the head, shoulder, and neck areas. It seldom comes to the ground. It feeds on young birds, lizards, and tree frogs. 145. Habitat. Found in dense rain forests and plantations. 146. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 75 centimeters. 147. Distribution. India, Burma, Malaya, Thailand, Laos, Cambodia, Vietnam, China, Indonesia, and Formosa. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 517 Survival 518 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards HABU PIT VIPER Trimeresurus flavoviridis 148. Description. Light brown or olive-yellow with black markings and a yellow or greenishwhite belly. 149. Characteristics. This snake is responsible for biting many humans and its bite could be fatal. It is an irritable species ready to defend itself. Its venom is hemotoxic, causing pain and considerable tissue damage. 150. Habitat. Found in a variety of habitats, ranging from lowlands to mountainous regions. Often encountered in old houses and rock walls surroundings buildings. 151. Length. Average 1 meter, maximum 1.5 meters. 152. Distribution. Okinawa and neighboring islands and Kyushu. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 519 Survival 520 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards HORNED DESERT VIPER Cerastes cerastes 153. Description. Pale buff color with obscure markings and a sharp spine (scale) over each eye. 154. Characteristics. As with all true vipers that live in the desert, it finds refuge by burrowing in the heat of the day, coming out at night to feed. It is difficult to detect when buried; therefore, many bites result from the snake being accidentally stepped on. Its venom is hemotoxic, causing severe damage to blood cells and tissue. 155. Habitat. Only found in very arid places within its range. 156. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 75 centimeters. 157. Distribution. Arabian Peninsula, Africa, Iran, and Iraq. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 521 Survival 522 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards KING COBRA Ophiophagus hannah 158. Description. Uniformly olive, brown, or green with ringlike crossbands of black. 159. Characteristics. Although it is the largest venomous snake in the world and it has a disposition to go with this honor, it causes relatively few bites on humans. It appears to have a degree of intelligence. It avoids attacking another venomous snake for fear of being bitten. It feeds exclusively on harmless species. The female builds a nest then deposits her eggs. Lying close by, she guards the nest and is highly aggressive toward anything that closely approaches the nest. Its venom is a powerful neurotoxin. Without medical aid, death is certain for its victims. 160. Habitat. Dense jungle and cultivated fields. 161. Length. Average 3.5 meters, maximum 5.5 meters. 162. Distibution. Thailand, southern China, Malaysia Peninsula, and Phillipines. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 523 Survival 524 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards KRAIT Bungarus caeruleus 163. Description. Black or bluish-black with white narrow crossbands and a narrow head. 164. Characteristics. Kraits are found only in Asia. This snake is of special concern to man. It is deadly—about 15 times more deadly than the common cobra It is active at night and relatively passive during the day. The native people often step on kraits while walking through their habitats. The krait has a tendency to seek shelter in sleeping bags, boots, and tents. Its venom is a powerful neurotoxin that causes respiratory failure. 165. Habitat. Open fields, human settlements, and dense jungle. 166. Length. Average 90 centimeters, maximum 1.5 meters. 167. Distribution. India, Sri Lanka, and Pakistan. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 525 Survival 526 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards LEVANT VIPER Vipera lebetina 168. Description. Gray to pale brown with large dark brown spots on the top of the black and a " ∧ " mark on top of the head. 169. Characteristics. This viper belongs to a large group of true vipers. Like its cousins, it is large and dangerous. Its venom is hemotoxic. Many deaths have been reported from bites of this species. It is a strong snake with an irritable disposition; it hisses loudly when ready to strike. 170. Habitat. Varies greatly, from farmlands to mountainous areas. 171. Length. Average 1 meter, maximum 1.5 meters. 172. Distribution. Greece, Iraq, Syria, Lebanon, Turkey, Afganistan, lower portion of the former USSR, and Saudi Arabia. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 527 Survival 528 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards MALAYAN PIT VIPER Callaselasma rhostoma 173. Description. Reddish running into pink tinge toward the belly with triangularshaped, brown markings bordered with Iight-colored scales. The base of the triangularshaped markings end at the midline. It has dark brown, arrow-shaped markings on the top and each side of its head. 174. Characteristics. This snake has long fangs, is ill-tempered, and is responsible for many bites. Its venom is hemotoxic, destroying blood cells and tissue, but a victim’s chances of survival are good with medical aid. This viper is a ground dweller that moves into many areas in search of food. The greatest danger is in stepping on the snake with bare feet. 175. Habitat. Rubber plantations, farms, rural villages, and rain forests. 176. Length. Average 60 centimeters, maximum 1 meter. 177. Distribution. Thailand, Laos, Cambodia, Java, Sumatra, Malaysia, Vietnam, Burma, and China. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 529 Survival 530 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards McMAHON'S VIPER Eristicophis macmahonii 178. Description. Sandy buff color dominates the body with darker brown spots on the side of the body. Its nose shield is broad, aiding in burrowing. 179. Characteristics. Very Iittle is known about this species. It apparently is rare or seldom seen. This viper is very irritable; it hisses, coils, and strikes at any intruder that ventures too close. Its venom is highly hemotoxic, causing great pain and tissue damage. 180. Habitat. Arid or semidesert. It hides during the day’s sun, coming out only at night to feed on rodents. 181. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 1 meter. 182. Distribution. West Pakistan and Afghanistan. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 531 Survival 532 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards MOLE VIPER OR BURROWING VIPER Atracaspis microlepidota 183. Description. Uniformly black or dark brown with a small, narrow head. 184. Characteristics. A viper that does not look like one. It is small in size, and its small head does not indicate the presence of venom glands. It has a rather inoffensive disposition; however, it will quickly turn and bite if restrained or touched. Its venom is a potent hemotoxin for such a small snake. Its fangs are exceptionally long. A bite can result even when picking it up behind the head. It is best to leave this snake alone. 185. Habitat. Agricultural areas and arid localities 186. Length. Average 55 centimeters, maximum 75 centimeters 187. Distribution. Sudan, Ethiopia, Somaliland, Kenya, Tanganyika, Uganda, Cameroon, Niger, Congo, and Urundi. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 533 Survival 534 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards PALESTINIAN VIPER Vipera palaestinae 188. Description. Olive to rusty brown with a dark V-shaped mark on the head and a brown, zigzag band along the back. 189. Characteristics. The Palestinian viper is closely related to the Russell’s viper of Asia. Like its cousin, it is extremely dangerous. It is active and aggressive at night but fairly placid during the day. When threatened or molested, it will tighten its coils, hiss loudly, and strike quickly. 190. Habitat. Arid regions, but may be found around barns and stables. It has been seen entering houses in search of rodents. 191. Length. Average 0.8 meter, maximum 1.3 meters. 192. Distribution. Turkey, Syria, Palestine, Israel, Lebanon, and Jordan. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 535 Survival 536 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards PUFF ADDER Bitis arietans 193. Description. Yellowish, light brown, or orange with chevron-shaped dark brown or black bars. 194. Characteristics. The puff adder is the second largest of the dangerous vipers. It is one of the most common snakes in Africa. It is largely nocturnal, hunting at night and seeking shelter during the day’s heat. It is not shy when approached. It draws its head close to its coils, makes a loud hissing sound, and is quick to strike any intruder. Its venom is strongly hemotoxic, destroying bloods cells and causing extensive tissue damage. 195. Habitat. And regions to swamps and dense forests. Common around human settlements. 196. Length. Average 12 meters, maximum 1.8 meters. 197. Distribution. Most of Africa, Saudi Arabia, Iraq, Lebanon, Israel, and Jordan. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 537 Survival 538 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards RHINOCEROS VIPER OR RIVER JACK Bitis nasicornis 198. Description. Brightly colored with purplish to reddish-brown markings and black and light olive markings along the back. On its head it has a triangular marking that starts at the tip of the nose. It has a pair of long horns (scales) on the tip of its nose. 199. Characteristics. Its appearance is awesome; its horns and very rough scales give it a sinister look. It has an irritable disposition. It is not aggressive but will stand its ground ready to strike if disturbed. Its venom is neurotoxic and hemotoxic. 200. Habitat. Rain forests, along waterways, and in swamps. 201. Length. Average 75 centimeters, maximum 1 meter. 202. Distribution. Equatorial Africa. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 539 Survival 540 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards RUSSELL’S VIPER Vipera russellii 203. Description. Light brown body with three rows of dark brown or black splotches bordered with white or yellow extending its entire length. 204. Characteristics. This dangerous species is abundant over its entire range. It is responsible for more human fatalities than any other venomous snake. It is irritable. When threatened, it coils tightly, hisses, and strikes with such speed that its victim has little chance of escaping. Its hemotoxic venom is a powerful coagulant, damaging tissue and blood cells. 205. Habitat. Variable, from farmlands to dense rain forests. It is commonly found around human settlements. 206. Length. Average 1 meter, maximum 1.5 meters 207. Distribution. Sri Lanka, south China, India, Malaysian Peninsula, Java, Sumatra, Borneo, and surrounding islands. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 541 Survival 542 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards SAND VIPER Cerastes vipera 208. Description. Usually uniformly very pallid, with three rows of darker brown spots 209. Characteristics. A very small desert dweller that can bury itself in the sand during the day’s heat. It is nocturnal, coming out at night to feed on lizards and small desert rodents. It has a short temper and will strike several times. Its venom is hemotoxic. 210. Habitat. Restricted to desert areas. 211. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 60 centimeters. 212. Distribution. Northern Sahara, Algeria, Egypt, Sudan, Nigeria, Chad, Somalia, and central Africa. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 543 Survival 544 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards SAW-SCALED VIPER Echis carinatus 213. Description. Color is light buff with shades of brown, dull red, or gray. Its sides have a white or lightcolored pattern. Its head usually has two dark stripes that start behind the eye and extend to the rear. 214. Characteristics. A small but extremely dangerous viper. It gets the name sawscaled from rubbing the sides of its body together, producing a rasping sound. This illtempered snake will attack any intruder. Its venom is highly hemotoxic and quite potent. Many deaths are attributed to this species. 215. Habitat. Found in a variety of environments. It is common in rural settlements, cultivated fields, arid regions, barns, and rock walls. 216. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 60 centimeters. 217. Distribution. Asia, Syria, India, Africa, Iraq, Iran, Saudi Arabia, Pakistan, Jordan, Lebanon, Sri Lanka, Algeria, Egypt, and Israel. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 545 Survival 546 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards WAGLER’S PIT VIPER OR TEMPLE VIPER Trimeresurus wagleri 218. Description. Green with white crossbands edged with blue or purple. It has two dorsal lines on both sides of its head. 219. Characteristics. It is also known as the temple viper because certain religious cults have placed venomous snakes in their temples. Bites are not uncommon for the species; fortunately, fatalities are very rare. It has long fangs. Its venom is hemotoxic causing cell and tissue destruction. It is an arboreal species and its bites often occur on the upper extremities. 220. Habitat. Dense rain forests, but often found near human settlements. 221. Length. Average 60 centimeters, maximum 100 centimeters. 222. Distribution. Malaysian Peninsula and Archipelago, Indonesia, Borneo, the Philippines. and Ryuku Islands. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 547 Survival 548 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards SECTION 8 POISONOUS SNAKES OF AUSTRALASIA AUSTRALIAN COPPERHEAD Denisonia superba 223. Description. Coloration is reddish brown to dark brown. A few from Queensland are black. 224. Characteristics. Rather sluggish disposition but will bite if stepped on. When angry, rears its head a few inches from the ground with its neck slightly arched. Its venom is neurotoxic. 225. Habitat. Swamps. 226. Length. Average 1.2 meters, maximum 1.8 meters. 227. Distribution. Tasmania, South Australia, Queensland, and Kangaroo Island. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 549 Survival 550 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards DEATH ADDER Acanthophis antarcticus 228. Description. Reddish, yellowish, or brown color with distinct dark brown crossbands The end of its tail is black, ending in a hard spine. 229. Characteristics. When aroused, this highly dangerous snake will flatten its entire body, ready to strike over a short distance. It is nocturnal, hiding by day and coming out to feed at night. Although it has the appearance of a viper, it is related to the cobra family. Its venom is a powerful neurotoxin; its causes mortality in about 50 percent of the victims, even with treatment. 230. Habitat. Usually found in arid regions, fields, and wooded lands. 231. Length. Average 45 centimeters, maximum 90 centimeters. 232. Distribution. Australia, New Guinea, and Moluccas. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 551 Survival 552 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards TAIPAN Oxyuranus scutellatus 233. Description. Generally uniformly olive or dark brown, the head is somewhat darker brown. 234. Characteristics. Considered one of the most deadly snakes. It has an aggressive disposition. When aroused, it can display a fearsome appearance by flattening its head, raising it off the ground, waving it back and forth, and suddenly striking with such speed that the victim may receive several bites before it retreats. Its venom is a powerful neurotoxin, causing respiratory paralysis. Its victim has little chance for recovery without prompt medical aid. 235. Habitat. At home in a variety of habitats, it is found from the savanna forests to the inland plains. 236. Length. Average 1.8 meters, maximum 3.7 meters. 237. Distribution. Northern Australia and southern New Guinea. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 553 Survival 554 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards TIGER SNAKE Notechis scutatus 238. Description. Olive to dark brown above with yellowish or olive belly and crossbands The subspecies in Tasmania and Victoria is uniformly black. 239. Characteristics. It is the most dangerous snake in Australia. It is very common and bites many humans. It has a very potent neurotoxic venom that attacks the nervous system. When aroused, it is aggressive and attacks any intruder. It flattens its neck making a narrow band. 240. Habitat. Found in many habitats from arid regions to human settlements along waterways to grasslands. 241. Length. Average 1.2 meters, maximum 1.8 meters. 242. Distribution. Australia, Tasmania, Bass Strait islands, and New Guinea. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 555 Survival 556 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards SECTION 9 POISONOUS SEA SNAKES BANDED SEA SNAKE Laticauda colubrina 243. Description. Smooth-scaled snake that is a pale shade of blue with black bands. Its oarlike tail provides propulsion in swimming. 244. Characteristics. Most active at night, swimming close to shore and at times entering tide pools. Its venom is a very strong neurotoxin. Its victims are usually fishermen who untangle these deadly snakes from large fish nets. 245. Habitat. Common in all oceans, absent in the Atlantic Ocean. 246. Length. Average 75 centimeters, maximum 1.2 meters. 247. Distribution. Coastal waters of New Guinea, Pacific islands, the Philippines, Southeast Asia, Sri Lanka, and Japan. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 557 Survival 558 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards YELLOW-BELLIED SEA SNAKE Pelamis platurus 248. Description. Upper part of body is black or dark brown and lower part is bright yellow. 249. Characteristics. A highly venomous snake belonging to the cobra family. This snake is truly of the pelagic species—it never leaves the water to come to shore. It has an oarlike tail to aid its swimming. This species is quick to defend itself. Sea snakes do not really strike, but deliberately turn and bite if molested. A small amount of their neurotoxic venom can cause death. 250. Habitat. Found in all oceans except the Atlantic Ocean. 251. Length. Average 0.7 meter, maximum 1.1 meters. 252. Distribution. Throughout the Pacific Ocean from many of the Pacific islands to Hawaii and to the coast of Costa Rica and Panama. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 559 Survival 560 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards SECTION 10 POISONOUS LIZARDS GILA MONSTER Heloderma suspectum 253. Description. Robust, with a large head and a heavy tail. Its body is covered with bead- Iike scales. It is capable of storing fat against lean times when food is scarce. Its color is striking in rich blacks laced with yellow or pinkish scales. 254. Characteristics. Not an aggressive lizard, but it is ready to defend itself when provoked. If approached too closely, it will turn toward the intruder with its mouth open. If it bites, it hangs on tenaciously and must be pried off. Its venom glands and grooved teeth are on its bottom jaw. 255. Habitat. Found in arid areas, coming out at night or early morning hours in search of small rodents and bird eggs. During the heat of the day it stays under brush or rocks. 256. Length. Average 30 centimeters, maximum 50 centimeters. 257. Distribution. Arizona, New Mexico, Utah, Nevada, northern Mexico, and extreme corner of southeast California. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 561 Survival 562 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix E Poisonous Snakes and Lizards MEXICAN BEADED LIZARD Heloderma horridum 258. Description. Less colorful than its cousin, the gila monster. It has black or pale yellow bands or is entirely black. 259. Characteristics. Very strong legs let this lizard crawl over rocks and dig burrows. It is short-tempered. It will turn and open its mouth in a threatening manner when molested. Its venom is hemotoxic and potentially dangerous to man. 260. Habitat. Found in arid or desert areas, often in rocky hillsides, coming out during evening and early morning hours. 261. Length. Average 60 centimeters, maximum 90 centimeters. 262. Distribution. Mexico through Central America. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 563 Survival 564 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Survival APPENDIX F DANGEROUS FISH AND MOLLUSKS 1. Since fish and mollusks may be one of your major sources of food, it is wise to know which ones are dangerous to you should you catch them. Know which ones are dangerous, what the dangers of the various fish are, what precautions to take, and what to do if you are injured by one of these fish. 2. Fish and mollusks will present a danger in one of three ways: by attacking and biting you, by injecting toxic venom into you through its venomous spines or tentacles, and through eating fish or mollusks whose flesh is toxic. 3. The danger of actually encountering one of these dangerous fish is relatively small, but it is still significant. Any one of these fish can kill you. Avoid them if at all possible. SECTION 1 FISH THAT ATTACK MAN 4. The shark is usually the first fish that comes to mind when considering fish that attack man. Other fish also fall in this category, such as the barracuda, the moray eel, and the piranha. SHARKS 5. Sharks are potentially the most dangerous fish that attack people. The obvious danger of sharks is that they are capable of seriously maiming or killing you with their bite. Of the many shark species, only a relative few are dangerous. Of these, four species are responsible for most cases of shark attacks on humans. These are the white, tiger, hammerhead, and blue sharks. There are also records of attacks by ground, gray nurse, and mako sharks. See Figure F-1 for illustrations of sharks. 6. Avoid sharks if at all possible. Follow the procedures discussed in Chapter 16 to defend yourself against a shark attack. 7. Sharks vary in size, but there is no relationship between the size of the shark and likelihood of attack. Even the smaller sharks can be dangerous, especially when they are traveling in schools. 8. If bitten by a shark, the most important measure for you to take is to stop the bleeding quickly. Blood in the water attracts sharks. Get yourself or the victim into a raft or to shore as soon as possible. If in the water, form a circle around the victim (if not alone), and stop the bleeding with a tourniquet. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 565 Survival Figure F-1: Sharks 566 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix F Dangerous Fish and Mullosks OTHER FEROCIOUS FISH 9. In salt water, other ferocious fish include the barracuda, sea bass, and moray eel (Figure F-2). The sea bass is usually an open water fish. It is dangerous due to its large size. It can remove large pieces of flesh from a human. Barracudas and moray eels have been known to attack man and inflict vicious bites. Be careful of these two species when near reefs and in shallow water. Moray eels are very aggressive when disturbed. Figure F-2: Ferocious Fish 10. In fresh water, piranha are the only significantly dangerous fish. They are inhabitants of the tropics and are restricted to northern South America. These fish are fairly small, about 5 to 7.5 centimeters, but they have very large teeth and travel in large schools. They can devour a 135-kilogram hog in minutes. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 567 Survival SECTION 2 VENOMOUS FISH AND INVERTEBRATES 11. There are several species of venomous fish and invertebrates, all of which live in salt water. All of these are capable of injecting poisonous venom through spines located in their fins, tentacles, or bites. Their venoms cause intense pain and are potentially fatal. If injured by one of these fish or invertebrates, treat the injury as for snakebite. 568 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix F Dangerous Fish and Mullosks STINGRAYS Dasyatidae SPECIES 12. RABBITFISH Siganidae SPECIES 13. Rabbitfish are found predominently on reefs in the Pacific and Indian oceans. They average about 30 centimeters long and have very sharp spines in their fins. The spines are venomous and can inflict intense pain. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 569 Survival SCORPION FISH OR ZIBRA FISH Scorpaeridae SPECIES 14. Scorpion fish or zebra fish live mainly in the reefs in the Pacific and Indian oceans. They vary from 30 to 90 centimeters long, are usually reddish in coloration, and have long, wavy fins and spines. They inflict an intensely painful sting. SIGANUS FISH 15. The siganus fish is small, about 10 to 15 centimeters long, and looks much like a small tuna. It has venomous spines in the dorsal and ventral fins. These spines can inflict painful stings. 570 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix F Dangerous Fish and Mullosks STONEFISH Synanceja SPECIES 16. Stonefish are found in the tropical waters of the Pacific and Indian oceans. Averaging about 30 centimeters in length, their subdued colors and lumpy shape provide them with exceptional camouflage. Whe stepped on, the fins in the dorsal spine inflice an extremely painful and sometimes fatal wound. TANG OR SURGEONFISH Acanthuridae SPECIES 17. Tang or surgeonfish average 20 to 25 centimeters in length, with a deep body, small mouth, and bright coloration. They have needlelike spines on the side of the tail that cause extremely painful wounds. This fish is found in all tropical waters. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 571 Survival TOADFISH Batrachoididae SPECIES 18. Toadfish are found in the tropical waters off the coasts of South and Central America. They are between 17.5 and 25 centimeters long and have a dull color and large mouths. They bury themselves in the sand and may be easily stepped on. They have very sharp, extremely poisonous spines on the dorsal fin (back). WEEVER FISH Trachinidae SPECIES 19. The weever fish is a tropical fish that is fairly slim and about 30 centimeters long. All fins have venomous spines that cause a painful wound. 572 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix F Dangerous Fish and Mullosks BLUE-RINGED OCTOPUS Hapalochlaena lunulata 20. This small octopus is usually found on the Great Barrier Reef off eastern Australia. It is grayish-white with iridescent blue ringlike markings. This octopus usually will not bite unless stepped on or handled. Its bite is extremely poisonous and frequently lethal. PORTUGUESE MAN-OF-WAR Physalis SPECIES 21. Although it resembles a jellyfish, the Portuguese man-of-war is actually a colony of sea animals. Mainly found in tropical regions, the Gulf Stream current can carry it as far as Europe. It is also found as far south as Australia. The floating portion of the manof-war may be as small as 15 centimeters, but the tentacles can reach 12 meters in length. These tentacles inflict a painful and incapacitating sting, but the sting is rarely fatal. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 573 Survival CONE SHELLS Conidae SPECIES 22. These cone-shaped shells have smooth, colorful mottling and long, narrow openings in the base of the shell. They live under rocks, in crevices or coral reefs, and along rocky shores of protected bays in tropical areas. All have tiny teeth that are similare to hypodermic needles. They can inject an extremely poisonous venom that acts very swiftly, causing acute pain, swelling, parlysis, blindness and possible death within hours. Avoid handling all cone shells. TEREBRA SHELLS Terebridae SPECIES 23. These shells are found in both temperate and tropical waters. They are similar to cone shells but much thinner and longer. The poison in the same way as cone shells, but the venom is not poisonous. 574 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix F Dangerous Fish and Mullosks SECTION 3 FISH WITH TOXIC FLESH 24. There are no simple rules to tell edible fish from those with poisonous flesh. The most common toxic fish are shown in Figure 8-2. All of these fish contain various types of poisonous substances or toxins in their flesh and are dangerous to eat. They have the following common characteristics: a. Most live in shallow water around reefs or lagoons. b. Many have boxy or round bodies with hard shell-like skins covered with bony plates or spines. They have small parrotlike mouths, small gills, and small or absent belly fins. Their names suggest their shape. 25. In addition to the above fish and their characteristics, barracuda and red snapper fish may carry ciguatera, a toxin that accumulates in the systems of fish that feed on tropical marine reefs. 26. Without specific local information, take the following precautions: a. Be very careful with fish taken from normally shallow lagoons with sandy or broken coral bottoms. Reef-feeding species predominate and some may be poisonous. b. Avoid poisonous fish on the leeward side of an island. This area of shallow water consists of patches of living corals mixed with open spaces and may extend seaward for some distance. Many different types of fish inhabit these shallow waters, some of which are poisonous. c. Do not eat fish caught in any area where the water is unnaturally discolored. This may be indicative of plankton that cause various types of toxicity in plankton-feeding fish. 27. Try fishing on the windward side or in deep passages leading from the open sea to the lagoon, but be careful of currents and waves. Live coral reefs drop off sharply into deep water and form a dividing line between the suspected fish of the shallows and the desirable deepwater species. Deepwater fish are usually not poisonous. You can catch the various toxic fish even in deep water. Discard all suspected reef fish, whether caught on the ocean or the reef side. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 575 Survival APPENDIX G CLOUDS: FORETELLERS OF WEATHER 1. About 200 years ago an Englishman classified clouds ac - cording to what they looked like to a person seeing them from the ground. He grouped them into three classes and gave them Latin names: cirrus, cumulus, and stratus. These three names, alone and combined with other Latin words, are still used to identify different cloud formations. 2. By being familiar with the different cloud formation and what weather they portend, you can take appropriate action for your protection. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 577 Survival CIRRUS CLOUDS 3. Cirrus clouds are the very high clouds that look like thin streaks or curls. They are usually 6 kilometers or more above the earth and are usually a sign of fair weather. In cold climates, however, cirrus clouds that begin to multiply and are accompanied by increasing winds blowing steadily from a northerly direction indicate an oncoming blizzard. CUMULUS CLOUDS 4. Cumulus clouds are fluffy, white, heaped-up clouds. These clouds, which are much lower than cirrus clouds, are often fair weather clouds. They are apt to appear around midday on a sunny day, looking like large cotton balls with flat bottoms. As the day advances, they may become bigger and push higher into the atmosphere. Piling up to appear like a mountain of clouds. These can turn into storm clouds. 578 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix G Clouds: Foretellers of Weather STRATUS CLOUDS 5. Stratus clouds are very low, gray clouds, often making an even gray layer over the whole sky. These clouds generally mean rain. NIMBUS CLOUDS 6. Nimbus clouds are ram clouds of uniform grayness that extend over the entire sky B-GL-382-006-FP-001 579 Survival CUMULONIMBUS CLOUDS 7. Cumulonimbus is the cloud formation resulting from a cumulus cloud building up, extending to great heights, and forming in the shape of an anvil. You can expect a thunderstorm if this cloud is moving in your direction. CIRROSTRATUS CLOUDS 8. Cirrostratus is a fairly uniform layer of high stratus clouds that are darker than cirrus clouds. Cirrostratus clouds indicate good weather. 580 B-GL-382-006-FP-001 Appendix G Clouds: Foretellers of Weather CIRROCUMULUS CLOUDS 9. Cirrocumulus is a small, white, round cloud at a high altitude. Cirrocumulus clouds indicate good weather. SCUDS 10. A loose, vapory cloud (scud) driven before the wind is a sign of continuing bad weather. B-GL-382-006-FP-001 581 Survival APPENDIX H CONTINGENCY PLAN OF ACTION FORMAT 1. Properly planning for the possible contingencies that may occur during a mission is a positive step toward being able to cope successfully with the changes in situation. The contingency plan of action is a critical document to an individual soldier, or to a unit, faced with evading enemy forces. First, it is a plan that will provide evaders a starting point to begin operating effectively once evasion has begun. Second, it gives recovery forces the ability to know what the evaders are planning to do, thus making recovery, operations easier. A well-thought-out contingency plan of action that everyone can understand is an important document to the evader. NOTE Upon deployment, you may carry with you the information compiled in A through E of the SITUATION paragraph only. I. SITUATION A. B. Country Climatic Zones 1. Tropical Rainy Climate 2. Dry Climate 3. Temperate Climate 4. Cold Climate (wet/dry) 5. Polar Climatic Land Zones (whatever is applicable) 1. Coasts – Seasons a. Temperature b. Precipitation c. General wind direction d. Cloud cover 2. Plains (refer to coasts) 3. Deserts (refer to coasts) B-GL-382-006-FP-001 583 Survival 4. Plateaus (refer to coasts) 5. Mountains (refer to coasts) 6. Swamps (refer to coasts) C. Light Data (BMNT, EENT, Moonrise, Moonset, Percent of Illumination) D. Terrain 1. Neighboring Borders 2. General Terrain Zones a.
